54 Years Ago, Peanuts Went From Meh To Masterpiece With Just One Comic

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Volumes have been written about what makes Peanuts great. And rightly so. But what about the moment when Peanuts became great? That's a different story. What was the comic where things really clicked for Charles M. Schulz? Where Peanuts leveled up and officially became what it was destined to be? It all goes back to a small but significant milestone for the strip's unexpected breakout hit character: Snoopy.

The Peanuts Gang never grew up. They might have celebrated birthdays now and then, but they never got older. They never matured. They did, however, evolve, especially early in the strip's 50-year run. And no character evolved faster, or more significantly, than Snoopy. The black-and-white beagle started out as a canine companion, a scrappy sidekick for Charlie Brown. By 2000, when Peanuts ended, Snoopy was the star of the show, and arguably had the most developed personality out of the whole Gang.

Over time, Snoopy's internal monologue became a Peanuts staple. It became familiar to readers to watch him react in his head, via thought balloon, to something Charlie Brown, or Lucy, or Linus just said. Or to carry on entire, one-sided conversations with himself. Or to lapse into fantasy entirely, imagining himself as a World War I pilot or a famous writer. Snoopy's interior life became more fleshed out than many of Peanuts' more ancillary characters.

To get to that point, there had to be a first time Snoopy had a thought. Or otherwise, the first time the audience was privy to what Snoopy was thinking. It might have just been a fun wrinkle for Charles Schulz to throw into the mix at the time, but it wound up having a profound effect on Peanuts, helping seal its place in pop culture in perpetuity.

It is May 1952. Peanuts has been running for about a year and a half. It is still trying to get its syndication numbers up to 50 newspapers across the United States. The strip has steady momentum. Back in January, it started running extra-sized Sunday strips, a signal that publisher United Features Syndicate and the editors at the papers that bought Peanuts believed in it.

It is a milestone year creatively too. Lucy van Pelt debuts in March. Linus, her brother, first appears later that fall. The catchphrase "good grief" pops up for the first time. The infamous "football gag" starts before the end of the year. And Snoopy, most of all, becomes a main cast member. That starts in May, when his interiority is depicted for the first time.

Originally, Snoopy looked quite different from the droopy-eared, peanut-headed icon he's known as today, starring in a beautifully minimalistic strip.

Before considering the moment everything changed for Snoopy, it is worth putting his look under the microscope. In '52, the beagle is still barely more than a doodle of a dog. He hasn't taken on his more recognizable form yet. That's still a few years off. So, to get all philosophical for a moment, like Peanuts was occasionally prone to, existence preceded essence for Snoopy, but once he gained the essential component of an interior monologue, he was finally able to evolve into the beloved pop culture icon that has existed ever since.

May 27, 1952. It is a Tuesday. Readers in 40-45 newspapers, from Allentown, Pennsylvania to Seattle, Washington open up the funny pages to find this strip. In the first frame, Snoopy sits serenely on the lawn, minding his own business. In the second panel, along comes Charlie Brown, humming some tune to himself. In panel three, Charlie Brown playfully tugs on Snoopy's ear and says, "kind of warm out today for ear muffs, isn't it?"

It is a dumb joke. A childish joke. Which makes sense. It is made by a child. But it is playful, and innocent enough. It is a joke, but it isn't this Peanuts strip's punchline. Instead, it is the set-up for the punchline, which comes in the final frame. For readers at the time, in '52, it would have been a delightful surprise. Snoopy can speak like a human! Or at least, think like one.

"Why do I have to suffer such indignities?" Snoopy thinks, walking away in a huff. The dog's thought bubble is a squiggly line, perhaps to convey that Snoopy is rattled by the interaction, or maybe just to visually differentiate it from the human Peanuts characters' dialogue. Either way, that stylistic touch wouldn't last, but what would is the portrayal of Snoopy as not just thinking complex human thoughts, but having complex human emotions.

Sure, Snoopy would become a bit more Zen as time went on, but he also demonstrated a temper at times. In this gamechanging Peanuts comic, he's indignant at having his idyllic moment interrupted. The foundations of Snoopy's long-term character had already been laid by the time Charles M. Schulz decided to give him an internal voice; that decision allowed Schulz to build Snoopy up from there in ways that never would have been possible otherwise.

Within a few years of Snoopy's first thought, Peanuts exploded in popularity. The strip went from appearing in dozens of newspapers to hundreds. It got media attention, as a result of its exponential growth and developing fandom. Snoopy's voice, and Snoopy's personality, were arguably the missing pieces of the puzzle that pushed Peanuts from good to great, from "meh" to masterpiece.

Another big moment for Snoopy in '52 was the first time he danced, something that would become a key part of his character when the Peanuts Gang made the jump to TV in the 1960s. It was another step toward humanizing him as a character. In turn, it was another level of depth added to Peanuts, opening up a whole new world of jokes for the series to make. So, this was a pivotal moment for more than just furthering the strip's appeal to readers. It was also critical to the longevity of Peanuts.

The Peanuts have many classic comics that are amazing, but here are seven comic strips related to Snoopy that are genuine 10/10 masterpieces.

It might not have been immediately evident as a "level up" moment for Peanuts, but in retrospect, it is the moment that Charles M. Schulz figured up the missing ingredient to his series and compensated. It was when Schulz solidified his place, and his characters' place, in the pop culture canon forever.

5 Student Loan Scams to Avoid in 2026

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

We believe everyone should be able to make financial decisions with confidence. While we don't cover every company or financial product on the market, we work hard to share a wide range of offers and objective editorial perspectives.

So how do we make money? Our partners compensate us for advertisements that appear on our site. This compensation helps us provide tools and services - like free credit score access and monitoring. With the exception of mortgage, home equity and other home-lending products or services, partner compensation is one of several factors that may affect which products we highlight and where they appear on our site. Other factors include your credit profile, product availability and proprietary website methodologies.

However, these factors do not influence our editors' opinions or ratings, which are based on independent research and analysis. Our partners cannot pay us to guarantee favorable reviews. Here is a list of our partners.

Student loan scams use pressure tactics and make false promises. Here’s what to watch out for.

Student loan scams can be costly, but they usually leave clues. Scammers will often try to hook victims with tempting claims of “act now or miss out” and “we promise to get you student aid."

The communication might seem realistic, but unexpected fees, unknown contacts and big promises are usually red flags. The age-old adage is true: if an offer sounds too good to be true, it probably is.

Here’s a breakdown of common student loan scams and what to do about them.

» MORE: How to spot student loan repayment scams

The scam: A fraudulent private company offers to manage your student loans and help you get them relieved or even fully forgiven. These services can cost more than $1,000, and there’s no guarantee that you’ll actually get the help you’re looking for.

How to avoid it: You can manage your federal student loans for free. According to the U.S. Department of Education, you never have to pay for help with your federal student loans[1]U.S. Department of Education. Avoiding Student Aid Scams. View all sources . You can work with your loan servicer to adjust your payment plan, consolidate loans and apply for loan forgiveness at no cost to you.

If you’re being promised immediate student loan forgiveness, are asked for personal identification information like your FAFSA login credentials or asked to authorize a power of attorney, proceed with caution — it’s likely a scam.

» MORE: How to get help with student loans

The scam: A company charges you an upfront fee to combine multiple student loans into one new loan to reduce your monthly payment. The company may be illegitimate and is likely to pocket the fee without actually consolidating your loans.

How to avoid it: You can consolidate federal student loans for free. There is no fee to consolidate your loans through the Department of Education at studentaid.gov. If you’re looking to reduce your monthly payment through consolidation, start by contacting your loan servicer. And if you get unsolicited communication from a company promising federal student loan consolidation, it's probably a scam.

The scam: Scammers use transitions in federal loan programs to take advantage of borrowers. For example, the Education Department was set to resume wage garnishments after a long pause, but in January 2026 announced a temporary delay implement repayment reforms.

When federal decisions go back and forth like this, it creates a gray area for scammers to make wage garnishment threats. For borrowers who may not know if wage garnishment restarted, it can be hard to know what to do. This goes for other federal changes, too; any uncertainty can be an opportunity for bad actors to take advantage of the confusion.

How to avoid it: If your wages are truly being garnished, the communication will come from the federal government and not a private company. Likewise, if a company offers to stop your wage garnishment for a fee, you’re probably getting scammed.

The scam: Criminals impersonate a real person and apply to colleges in their name. Then, they can collect financial aid, take out loans and use their college admission to net discounts on goods and services. This is known as a “ghost student” scam.

How to avoid it: Follow the steps on identitytheft.gov to report fraud and get a recovery plan. There are a few safeguards you can put in place to prevent this form of identity theft, such as setting up a credit freeze and monitoring your FSA ID activity.

How it works: You might receive a call from someone claiming to be from your school’s financial aid office saying you have unpaid tuition. The caller may threaten that you cannot start your classes until your tuition bill is paid in full. Scammers can be creative and convincing, even going so far as to replicate a school’s phone number to display on your caller ID.

How to avoid it: The Federal Trade Commission warns that schools will not call and pressure you to pay tuition[2]Federal Trade Commission. Got a call about unpaid college tuition? It could be a scam. Accessed Nov 16, 2026. View all sources . Rather than trust the caller, use your school's secure student portal to check your account balance. Know for certain what you owe and to whom before sending money. You can also call your financial aid office to confirm the legitimacy of a phone number that has called you requesting tuition funds.

Realizing you’ve been scammed is stressful, and it’s natural to feel overwhelmed or embarrassed. But acting quickly can make a real difference. The sooner you report what happened, the better your chances of limiting the financial damage and getting your accounts back on track. Here’s what to do:

If you’ve already paid a scammer, report the problem to the bank that issued the payment. If you paid with a credit card, for instance, contact the company and tell them what happened. They may be able to freeze your account or reverse the transaction.

Reach out to your federal student loan servicer as well and report what happened. They can check to make sure no changes have been made to your account and, in the case that changes did occur, they can help you get your loan back on track.

File complaints with the Education Department, the Federal Trade Commission and the Consumer Financial Protection Bureau. Filing complaints with these agencies helps hold scammers accountable, can result in enforcement action or business shutdowns, and in some cases may help you recover lost funds.

Mormon Wives' Bobbi Althoff Defends Costar McCall DaPron From Critics

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Mormon Wives: OC star Bobbi Althoff spoke out on costar McCall DaPron's "mistakes" as she faced criticism from viewers for her treatment of her castmates, including Aspyn Ovard, on the show.

Bobbi Althoff means business when it comes to defending her friends.

The Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: OC star is sticking up for costar McCall DaPron, who faced backlash for questioning Althoff's religious background as a non-Mormon and whether Aspyn Ovard had forged relationships in their inner circle for clout.

"I'm going to need all you people to stop bullying McCall. It's really out of control," the reality star penned on an Instagram Story Nov. 15. "I understand she made some mistakes but your response to calling her a mean girl is being mean?"

Though Althoff and DaPron—the older sister of SLOMW's Mayci Neeley, who formed a friendship with Ovard during the show—bumped heads, the podcaster said the audience giving her castmate the villain treatment online is "not necessary," as she and DaPron, 36, made amends since filming some of those tense moments.

"People make mistakes. People learn from them & people grow," Althoff, 29, continued. "Imagine having your lowest most vulnerable moments out there for everyone to see."

And fellow Mormon Wives star Avery Woods agreed that DaPron had grown from her missteps, praising her costar's "incredible come around moment at the end of the season" in a group interview with Entertainment Tonight shared Nov. 13. Their reconciliation followed a conflict in episode two, when DaPron tried to share a negative comment left by one of Woods' followers after the influencer had explained she avoids reading mean posts on social media.

As such, Althoff advised fans to remember to keep perspective as her new friend group finds their footing.

In other words, "Just be patient & kind and have grace because we are all human."

For her part, DaPron denied her and Jordan McClanahan's status as the series' antagonists.

"I don't feel like we're the villains. Maybe that's how we were portrayed," she told the outlet in the same joint interview. "So much is taken out of context or a lot gets cut."

That said, the jewelry designer—who shares four kids with husband Alex DaPron—said watching the season back was "all very confusing."

And it's no secret Mormon Wives has seen its fair share of drama, not only between the women, but within romantic relationships, too. Read on for where each of the spinoff's couples stand today.

Following her divorce from Cory Althoff, podcaster Bobbi Althoff is dating Tyler Hawkins, who plays a special role in Secret Lives of Mormon Wives: Orange County.

"He styles me, that’s how he supports me,” she told E! News in May, adding that the basketball player, "picks out my outfits every day. He styles me every day for the show. It’s great."

Aspyn Ovard, who filed for divorce from Parker Ferris in 2024 after eight years of marriage, is currently in a throuple relationship with married couple Billy Davis and Bri Davis.

After many ups and downs, Jen Affleck—who is pregnant with her and Zac Affleck's fourth baby—officially filed for divorce from her husband of seven years, E! News confirmed Nov. 4.

Despite ongoing relationship speculation, Salomé Andrea and Aaron Brimhall are still going strong as they prepare to welcome their third baby together. The duo, who married in 2012, also share 8-year-old twins Luca and Rue.

McCall DaPron—the sister of Mormon Wives' Mayci Neeley—shares four kids with husband Kirk DaPron, who she wed in 2011.

Ashleigh Pease and Nate Pease are rock solid after over a decade of marriage. "This 13th year together is one that has felt full circle!" she wrote on Instagram in September. "A season of fruits. Comfort. The happiest milestones."

Madison Bontempo and Kyler Steven Fisher, who wed in 2013, have been open about the highs and lows of their relationship. "Kyler and I aren’t perfect," she wrote on Instagram in October. "We’ve had arguments. We’ve had hard seasons. We’ve had moments we wish we could take back. And we’re not afraid to admit that."

But, she added, "Those moments aren’t our whole story. There’s also laughter, friendship, forgiveness, growth, five beautiful children, a million memories, and a love that has continued to evolve through every season of our lives."

Avery Woods and David Woods, parents to Ziggy and Stevie, are still feeling the love after a decade of marriage. As she simply put it, "my forever + always."

Apple TV’s Masterpiece Spy Thriller Series Is So Good, It’s Renewed For 4 More Seasons

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Apple TV's spy thriller series has scored an unprecedented win on streaming, with four more seasons confirmed to be on the way, and it just proves how the show is unmatched when it comes to the genre. The espionage genre has become the talk of the town lately, as all eyes turned to one of the biggest movie franchises of all time.

After all, Dune director Denis Villeneuve was charged with casting Daniel Craig's replacement as James Bond for the suave spy's new movie. After a long casting process with many rumors and conflicting reports, The Batman star Robert Pattinson has been officially announced to play James Bond. That reveal comes on the same day that one of Apple TV's best shows released its own exciting future update.

In a competitive streaming market where espionage thrillers have become some of the most popular shows around, Apple TV continues to thrive with a long-running British spy series. The show in question faces competition from hits like Michael Fassbender's The Agency, Gabriel Basso's The Night Agent, Richard Madden's Citadel, John Krasinski's Tom Clancy's Jack Ryan, and more genre successes.

However, only Slow Horses has managed to go the distance with six seasons so far, and all of them being released on an annual schedule. In fact, the first two seasons of Slow Horses arrived on Apple TV in the same year, 2022, showing how the spy thriller differs from its genre competition. Now, Slow Horses just got a massive future update that further sets it apart from other espionage TV shows.

The Slow Horses season 6 finale aired on October 21, but the show's future was already confirmed to extend well beyond its latest season. After all, Apple TV renewed Slow Horses for season 7 in July 2025, while the hit show was confirmed to come back for season 8 more recently, in September, right before Slow Horses season 6 was released on the streaming service. Based on the Mick Herron series of novels following the agents of Slough House, seasons 7 and 8 will be based on the books Bad Actors and Clown Town, respectively, but that is not all.

Slow Horses boasts a near-perfect 97% overall critics' score on Rotten Tomatoes for its six seasons.

Recently, Sid Baker actress Olivia Cooke, who returned to the show in season 6 after a prolonged absence, indicated that Slow Horses might call it a day after season 8. Talking to The Times, Cooke claimed she was shooting the final season of the Apple TV spy thriller series and that the show coming to an end was "what we’ve been told." However, the future could not be brighter for fans of the espionage drama, as Apple TV has officially confirmed that Slow Horses has been renewed for seasons 9 and 10. That said, it means a major change is happening.

So far, all seasons of Slow Horses have had source material to adapt, mostly covering a single book of Herron's fan-favorite series of novels. However, that franchise runs out of books to adapt with the upcoming release of season 8, which should happen in 2028, if the show keeps to its annual release schedule. While Herron has plans to write more books in the franchise, he has a tighter deadline now in place if they are to be adapted, with seasons 9 and 10 likely to begin filming soon, given how Slow Horses season 8 filming is underway two years before its release.

Besides running out of source material to adapt, Slow Horses seasons 9 and 10 could have had to deal with another major change, as the presence of Jack Lowden's River Cartwright was uncertain when the series' renewal was announced. After all, the actor was a finalist for the role of the next James Bond, and if he got the role, that likely meant Slow Horses losing River, a key part of the show throughout all seasons. However, with Robert Pattinson as the new 007, Lowden is expected to remain as Slow Horses works through its four upcoming seasons.

8 War Movie Masterpieces Praised By Real-Life Veterans

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

War movies movies have been a consistent genre throughout the history of cinema. Hollywood has repeatedly tapped into the stories of real-life conflicts, showing dramatized versions of what really happened. These efforts came with great rewards, too, as countless war movies have been hugely successful at the worldwide box office.

Audiences typically look for a lot of things when choosing their next war movie to watch — action, drama between characters, and potentially world-defining stakes. Yet, there’s another element that typically comes into play, too, and that is what real-life veterans think of a given film. After all, getting their stamp of approval is mightily important to any war movie, as it shows that the story they told is (for the most part) accurate to what really happened.

Some war movies have been praised for their portrayal of the conflict at hand. Andrew Garfield’s Hacksaw Ridge, which tells the story of a soldier who, due to his strong religious beliefs, opts not to carry a weapon of any kind during battle, is one war film that has been praised repeatedly. These accolades have come not only from historians who have spent their entire careers studying the conflict, but veterans who were actually there, too.

War films praised by veterans aren’t just limited to World War II, either. Movies that have centered on more recent conflicts, such as the wars in the Middle East, have also been lauded by those who served for their accuracy of their portrayal of history. Regardless, war movies remain an incredibly popular subgenre in Hollywood today, and if they’re praised by real-life veterans, then chances go up that it’s an excellent film as well.

Andrew Garfield’s Hacksaw Ridge is one war movie that has consistently been praised by real-life veterans. As stated, the film tells the story of Desmond Doss, a Seventh-Day Adventist who objected to conflict of any kind. However, when drafted into the United States military, he was forced to either forgo his religion or risk criminal punishment for not fighting.

Miraculously, Doss found a solution to this problem — not carrying a weapon at all. During combat, such as at the Battle of Okinawa (he later received a Medal of Honor for his actions during the battle), Doss had no weapons on him. He had no rifle, no pistol, and nothing that could cause harm to another human being, as Doss was adamant that he wouldn’t hurt anyone. Instead, he served as a medic, thus saving the lives of those on the battlefield, and earning a reputation as one of the bravest soldiers in history in the process.

When Hacksaw Ridge released in theaters in 2016, the question naturally arose about how much of the film’s story was accurate to real events. After all, while some war movies are based in realism, others also take plenty of story liberties. Garfield’s Hacksaw Ridge wasn’t one of these films, however, and has instead been praised by several veterans.

For example, the film’s director, Mel Gibson, showed the finished product at the Disabled American Veterans national convention in Atlanta, Georgia. The film received great applause upon its conclusion, with many veterans praising its accuracy. Dan Clare, an Iraqi war veteran, noted that Hacksaw Ridge was a surprise hit with the crowd.

“These were people who avoid war movies because they fear they will either be unrealistic or that they will trigger post-traumatic stress. To their surprise, most people felt that it was a realistic depiction of combat — one of the most accurate ever — and, by and large, they appreciated the way it portrayed PTSD. Bottom line? Unlike most Hollywood movies, this one felt like it represented us.”

Ridley Scott’s 2001 film Black Hawk Down tells the story of two Black Hawk helicopters that were shot down during the Battle of Mogadishu. Once again, this film was praised for its depiction of the battle that its based on, with many veterans who fought during this conflict noting the film’s accuracy. Not only that, but one veteran stated that “90–95% of the film” is accurate, a high number that shows just how much attention to detail went into this movie.

Another more recent title, The Outpost is based on a non-fiction book by newscaster Jake Tapper. The film tells the story of the Battle of Kamdesh and the United States soldiers stationed at the Combat Outpost (COP) Keating in Afghanistan. A fatal design flaw of the base meant that the soldiers were vulnerable to ambush from the surrounding mountains, and the film then shows their desperate fight to survive.

One of the things that makes The Outpost such a unique film is that it not only received praise from veterans, but was actually worked on by veterans, too. Director Rod Lurie is a graduate of West Point and served in the U.S. Army, and not only that, but the film also recruited several survivors from the actual events to serve as advisers on the story. This helps to make The Outpost feel raw, lived in, and true to real-life events.

Another recent film with plenty of veteran involvement is Warfare, which tells the story of an encounter that a group of Marines had shortly after the Battle of Ramadi in 2006. Interestingly, the film is told in real time, with each second passing at the same rate for both the audience and the characters. Not only that, but the script was also based solely on personal accounts that survivors gave after the attack occurred.

These methods were praised by veterans, including survivors, for adding to the film’s overall authenticity. In particular, veterans praised the “smaller moments” for depicting the quiet moments of battle, which many war films skip over. The performances of the cast were also praised, as actors like Joseph Quinn and Will Poulter did extensive research to make their portrayals as accurate as possible.

One of the earliest films on this list is The Best Years of Our Lives, which released just one year after the conclusion of World War II, in 1946. The film tells the story of three United States soldiers who return to their small towns after serving, but find the transition back to civilian life especially difficult. For example, Al Stephenson (played by Frederic March) struggles with alcoholism upon his return and, at the same time, also has a very difficult job at a bank, in which he is given the task of approving or denying loans for fellow veterans.

The Best Years of Our Lives is timeless in this regard. The film may focus on World War II veterans, but it can just as easily translate to virtually every conflict in modern human history, with soldiers struggling to find their footing after returning home. The realistic-ness of this portrayal was praised by veterans upon its release, many of whom felt seen by the film. Amazingly, many of the film’s actors were veterans themselves, including Harold Russell, who, just like his character, lost both his hands during the war.

The head of Veterans Affairs at the time, General Omar Bradley, praised this film upon its release. In fact, he was such a fan of the movie that he organized a showing for VA headquarters staff, and later went on to write to the film’s producer to express his appreciation.

Steven Spielberg has directed so many iconic films in his career, and Saving Private Ryan is right up there among the very best. The film, which released in 1998, tells the story of a battalion of U.S. soldiers who set out on a mission to rescue Private James Francis Ryan (after his brothers had already tragically been lost in battle). Tom Hanks plays Captain John Miller, the leader of the rescue mission, and Matt Damon takes on the role of Private Ryan.

Many World War II veterans, some of whom actually fought in the specific battles that Saving Private Ryan depicts, praised Spielberg’s film. D-Day veterans noted that the Omaha Beach sequence captured the true violence of the war, with paratrooper Jack Evans saying: “A lot of people will see this and think it's gruesome, but that's the way it was. There were a lot of times it was a lot worse."

One of the most recent films on this list, Atonement tells the true story of a deadly battle during the Iraq War and its longstanding aftermath. Starring Kenneth Branagh and Boyd Holbrook, Atonement begins with an extended sequence showing American soldiers patrolling the streets of a populated Iraqi village. They hear gunfire, however, and this quickly sends them into action.

On the civilian side of things, an Iraqi family similarly hears the gunfire and decides to make a run for it and escape to their hometown. What happens, though, ends up being a tragedy in every sense of the word, as the family is caught in the crossfire between Iraqi militants and the U.S. Army. Their attempts to inform the latter that they aren’t a threat prove to be fatal, however, as several of the family members are killed by U.S. soldiers, who mistakenly think of them as a threat.

Atonement is based directly not just on real events, but on real people, too. The Khachaturian family were the ones impacted by this tragedy, and Boyd Holbrook’s Lou D'Alessandro was (one of) the real soldiers in question. Meanwhile, Kenneth Branagh’s Michael Reid was the journalist who covered the events (and who facilitated a meeting between both parties years later).

When the film premiered at the Toronto International Film Festival in September, director Reed Van Dyk stated that he wanted to honor those in this story by depicting the events as accurately as possible. During the Q&A after the film’s premiere, Van Dyk noted that he and his team had received the stamp of approval from veterans who served during the War in Iraq, with particular praise going to the intensity of the combat sequence that then sets up the rest of the movie.

The 1993 film Gettysburg tells the story of the Battle of Gettysburg during the American Civil War in the mid-1800s. The film, which is written and directed by Ronald F. Maxwell, was originally filmed as a miniseries but was released for a limited run in theaters after positive reception. Because of this, it remains the longest film released in theaters in U.S. history.

Because of how long ago this war was, no direct veterans were around to see this movie. However, veterans of future conflicts noted that Gettysburg has a surprising commitment to depicting all aspects of war. Not only that, but historians praised Maxwell for including real battlefield formations, re-enactors, and for filming near the real locations where key battles took place.

Harvard and Dartmouth Keep Bitcoin ETF Share Counts Steady as Q3 Values Rise

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

Two university investment portfolios offer a quieter picture of institutional Bitcoin exposure than the usual buying-and-selling headlines. Harvard and Dartmouth reported the same numbers of shares in BlackRock’s Bitcoin ETF at the end of September as they had at the end of June. The positions became more valuable as the fund rose.

The distinction is useful: a larger dollar figure can describe a change in market prices without showing an increase in the number of shares held. These filings, submitted on November 13, also reveal other portfolio choices that put the Bitcoin positions into perspective.

Harvard Management Company’s September filing lists 3,044,612 shares of the iShares Bitcoin Trust ETF, valued at $144.1 million. Dartmouth’s disclosure lists 201,531 shares worth approximately $9.5 million.

The corresponding June disclosures show identical share counts, valued at about $101.4 million and $6.7 million. That works out to an increase of roughly 42% in the reported value of each position. The calculation compares the two filing snapshots; it is not a measure of fresh cash invested during the quarter.

Matching endpoints do not establish that no transactions happened between them. A manager could trade during a quarter and finish with the same holding. The documents establish the reported balances on the two dates.

The difference is easier to see in Harvard’s SPDR Gold Shares position. Its latest filing reports 1,161,000 GLD shares, compared with 58,862 in the June information table. That is nearly twenty times the earlier share count. The September position was worth about $442.2 million.

Those figures support a statement that Harvard’s reported exposure through that gold fund expanded substantially. They do not, by themselves, explain why. Inflation protection, portfolio construction and other motives require evidence beyond a list of securities.

Nor does the gold increase prove that Bitcoin financed it. Establishing such a rotation would require information about cash movements and the wider portfolio. Here, the Bitcoin share count stayed level while the GLD count rose.

Dartmouth also reported 304,803 shares in the Bitwise Solana Staking ETF and 178,148 shares in the Grayscale Ethereum Staking ETF. Those share counts likewise match its June table. Their September values were approximately $4.9 million and $4.5 million respectively.

The fund names describe distinct underlying exposures. Combining their dollar values can show the size of the reported crypto-linked positions, but it does not turn them into one interchangeable investment. Bitcoin, Ether and Solana can respond differently to network developments, and a staking product introduces mechanics that a straightforward Bitcoin trust does not have.

NewsBTC’s recent report on JPMorgan’s crypto-linked securities holdings shows another reason to inspect the actual instrument before interpreting an institutional total.

The SEC’s Form 13F guidance explains the delayed quarterly reporting framework. These disclosures are a record of reportable securities holdings, not a complete picture of each university’s assets or a live trading feed.

That timing matters after November’s sustained Bitcoin ETF withdrawals. A September position can coexist with subsequent market selling, and the latest filings cannot show whether either university changed its exposure after quarter-end.

The evidence is specific but useful: both reported stable Bitcoin ETF share counts through September, while Harvard’s gold holding expanded sharply. The next comparable filing will show whether that pattern survived the market’s later reversal.

Soneil Spark introduces its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

This week, Soneil Spark rolls out its Mobile Battery Energy Storage System (Mobile BESS), the portable, modular battery storage built into a towable trailer.

The company, offering bespoke, modular energy solutions, is introducing Mobile BESS at RE+ 2026. It delivers temporary backup and off-grid power, working with what’s available by taking power from the grid, a generator, or solar.

“Wherever your business goes, the power goes with it. Simply tow it where energy demand grows,” said Sonya Jain, CEO of Soneil Spark, in a statement. “Start with one unit and add more as your needs change.”

It’s a simple set up, the company said. Mobile BESS works with no crane, no trenching, and no utility wait. That makes it a natural fit for a job site which may be months away from permanent service, a film set or event that can’t tolerate generator noise and fumes, a utility crew restoring power after a storm, or a remote mine or farm beyond the grid’s reach.

On sites that already run generators, the Mobile BESS syncs with them to cover peaks and start-up gaps, so the generators run fewer hours. Where diesel is restricted, it offers a quiet alternative.

Where containerized storage is built to be placed once, the Mobile BESS is built to be towed and to work in any weather. It includes:

The Mobile BESS is built for generator and equipment rental, construction, emergency and backup power, utilities, electrical contractors, events, film and entertainment, and off-grid sites such as mining and agriculture. A hybrid option adds EV charging.

Exec: Time for Banks to Reclaim Mortgage Biz From Non-Bank Lenders

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Mortgage rates recently reached 7.51% on the 30-year fixed, almost a full percentage point higher than last year. Despite that, the CEO of Chase Home Lending plans to aggressively expand its mortgage business to reclaim market share from non-bank lenders.

In a recent report on CNBC, Sean Grzebin noted that while banks had to recover consumer confidence following the great financial crisis of 20-some years ago, he thinks that’s a done deal.

“The non-banks are all very formidable competitors, and I think they’ve had an advantage while the banks were distracted through several decades,” he said. “Now you’re starting to see the resurgence of banks. A lot of banks are actually going to grow their mortgage business through this.”

Non-banks currently dominate originations with a 66.3% share by dollar volume, up from just 27% before the 2008 financial crisis, according to CNBC. However, large banks with $50 billion or more in assets are now increasing originations by 34% annually, outperforming competitors.

“We’re going to hire a bunch of lending advisors across the United States,” said Grzebin. “We’re going to focus on areas where we haven’t had a lot of coverage, but there’s a lot of mortgage activity and transactions taking place. And it really matches the expansion that we’re having within the branch network on the consumer bank side.”

Non-banks had a 66.3% share of originations by dollar volume in the first half of this year, an increase over all of 2025, according to Inside Mortgage Finance. That compares with just a 27% share in 2000 before the financial crisis. But large banks, those with $50 billion and more in assets, are now increasing originations by 34% on an annual basis, outperforming both competing banks and non-banks.”

Why scammers try to keep you on the phone (and how to spot their tactics)

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

If something feels off, hang up and call the company back on their official number.

That unexpected call from someone claiming to be from your bank can be worrisome, especially if they're calling to alert you about a suspicious money transfer. Your savings are at risk, but fret not, the caller who somehow got hold of your number says they can stop it — as long as you stay on the line.

But more than the emergency itself, their instruction to stay on call is the real catch. Scammers are counting on you to not hang up, as that valuable time helps them earn your trust. It then leads to turning up the pressure, which eventually leads you toward a decision you otherwise wouldn't take. That's when they might ask for a verification code, or straight up ask you to make a transfer. Keeping you occupied just long enough is the plan, so you don't end up calling the bank for a second opinion.

Dedicated scammers are known to stay on the line as the victims login to the accounts to transfer funds, buy gift cards or make a visit to the nearby ATM to withdraw cash. Some even go as far as to school you on what to tell bank employees when questioned about that odd transaction. When the person "helping" you protect your savings is deterring you from speaking to anyone else, that's a pretty obvious sign that something is off.

One possible way in could be a compromised bank account. A creative scammer could inform you about open accounts under your name or even scare you with an arrest warrant. The plan is to make you feel that following their instructions is the only way out, so hanging up doesn't feel like a plausible option.

Sometimes, the whole script includes more than just one scam artist. Back in August 2026, the FBI reported that scammers posing as bank employees told their victims that their information had been used to open an account to purchase illegal firearms. The scammers then transferred the victims to someone posing as an FBI agent, but disconnected after the victims were encouraged to verify the number.

Shortly after, they received a call displaying the FBI's Boston office number, which had been spoofed. These "FBI agents" could then ask the victims to continue the conversations using an encrypted messaging app. The FBI didn't specify how many people were targeted by this scheme.

But the threatening villain persona is not the only way these scammers are out to get you. Some of them act professional, stay patient and even go a step further to sound sympathetic. Now, when these friendly voices start to discourage you from reaching out to the organization directly or getting a second opinion, that should get your alarm bells ringing. After all, con artists don't want all of their efforts to result in failure.

Once the small talk is done, call handlers may switch to a dictating tone in order to get the outcome they want. This is a clear warning sign. When the caller demands you stay connected or instructs you to keep the conversation private, be sure to trust your instincts and end the call.

When they ask you to move funds to a "safer" account, it's time to hang up. Any request for passwords or even one-time verification codes should also be met with a call termination. Since scammers are often savvy enough to spoof authentic phone numbers, caller ID isn't all that reliable either.

Once you hang up or end that text conversation, reach out to the organization, but only using its official app or the number on your bank card or statement. Steer clear of contact numbers that the caller gives you, and trusting the customer-service numbers from sponsored search results isn't safe either. If it's a bit late to heed this advice and you've already shared sensitive information or worse transferred money to the scammer, contact your bank or payment provider right away.

Did you know your phone can filter out some of these unwanted calls? On iPhones running iOS 26 or newer, head to Settings > Apps > Phone > Screen Unknown Callers to enable extra incoming call checks, which include silencing unsaved numbers or asking callers to state who they are before they're connected.

If you own a supported Samsung Galaxy phone, try out spam filtering under Phone > Settings > Caller ID and spam protection, and Google Pixel owners can set up Call Screen using the Phone app's settings on supported devices in eligible regions. The downside is that legitimate callers can sometimes get caught by these filters.

Companies like Meta, Microsoft and Starlink have already worked to help take down over a million scam accounts. If you want to help save other unsuspecting consumers, it's best to report suspected scams to the FTC.

Personal Finance (Not Investing) • Re: Should I walk away from my career and pursue a custodian position?

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

It sounds like you’ve been job-hunting for a month. That’s not that long in the grand scheme of things. If you’re in a less populated area, there are likely fewer positions, but they do crop up over a longer period of time (6 months to a year). I understand that the limited timeline of severance and unemployment is stressful, but you’re really at the start of the process.
That is true.
There’s nothing wrong with a career pivot at your point in life, but I think there’s some key details you’re glossing over. First and foremost, repetitive strain is tough on the body whether it’s sitting in a desk chair or mopping the floor. If you don’t have recent experience with this type of work, it is very easy to develop a strain or injury and if you’re the only one on shift, you will be expected to deal with it, most likely by working through it.
You raise a very good point. I was 18 when I worked my last custodial position. While I'm in decent shape for my age, I'm not as flexible as I was then and am more injury prone now.
Second shift can also be tough if you want to attend personal or networking functions after typical 9-5 hours. 3-11 is not too bad if you want to continue searching for outside work, since you can still interview during morning hours, but I would not expect much from internal opportunities.
That's not really an issue for me. I would always feel bad about requesting time off for appointments during the day.
Most administrators will have very little overlap with you and therefore be relatively unable to notice or vouch for your abilities. It’s honestly the worst of all worlds, because you deal with the bias against your current position while not having any internal networking benefits.

I also think you underestimate how much stress there can be. People will see the results of your work and may pass critical feedback through the grapevine (your supervisor). You won’t have any chance to clarify or rebut this feedback. You are unlikely to receive positive feedback or appreciation when things go right.
Those are some potential downsides. Thanks for bringing them up!

Personal Investments • Re: How to incorporate an inheritance into retirement plan??

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →


This ^ is simply guesswork. You don't know when they'll each die, and you don't know what their respective estates will be worth at that unknown future date. Do you even know what percentage of the estate(s) you'll be receiving?

Regards,
Yes we know exactly the percentage and the current value. We know where and how the money is invested. The only things we do not know is the day of death and the future performance of the market. Hey that sounds like every single person's retirement now doesn't it?

So I find it somewhat odd that people simply say do not count on it because you don't know when and how the markets perform. If we truly take that approach none of us should retire because we don't know when we will die nor how the markets will perform. Yet we still do and plan with some sort of factor of safety such as a slightly lower assumed rate of return, the understanding we can change our spending in the future, etc. So why can't we make some sort of safety factor assumptions for inheritance especially if we know certain facts such as current age, amounts, investment types etc.?

I mentioned my safety factors such as assuming they live to 102 and assuming the amount is 50%. By making this type of assumption and modeling that income out in the future, would permit us to spend today at a higher rate of out current account balance. This isn't so that we can be spoiled as our retirement portfolio will afford us a comfortable retirement but it would permit us to enjoy a little more a little sooner or to convey those blessing on others sooner.
I think your logic here is a bit faulty. You can't compare the unknowns of some future inheritance to the unknowns of your portfolio, because the risks are not equivalent.

You would not begin withdrawing 15% of your portfolio every year because the results might be catastrophic. But an inheritance is an addition to your own savings... it's not your baseline. So, yes, your inheritance might be 50% of what's expected, or it might be 10%, ir it might be 110%. But regardless, in either scenario you won't end up destitute, assuming you are careful with your own money. And, there's the black swan event: parent remarries, parent gets swindled, parent needs massive heathcare spending, and so on. If you overspend and get nothing, you're in trouble.

Is it reasonable to assume some (reduced) level of inheritance? Yes. By doing so, is it wise to begin drawing down your portfolio beyond what is prudent? I'd say no. If you want the money, there are ways to get it: The parent can gift you, your spouse, and each of your kids a lot of money, every year, forever. Probably more than you'd spend annually based on your "50% reduction" scenario.

My SO and I stand to inherit 8 figures from parents who are in their late 80s. We are still saving.

ES Foundry ships more than 1 GW of U.S.-manufactured solar cells

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

ES Foundry, a U.S. manufacturer of high-efficiency solar cells, is at RE+ 2026 this week to showcase its American-made solar cells and announce a milestone in its operating capacity.

According to the company, it is the first and only crystalline silicon solar cell manufacturer to ship more than 1 gigawatt (GW) of domestically manufactured solar cells in the United States.

As one of the few U.S. solar cell manufacturers with a fully operational facility producing and shipping cells to customers, ES Foundry is helping move American solar manufacturing to commercial reality.

In July, the company announced it reached its full 3-GW manufacturing capacity. The company began production on its first 1-GW line last year and has now completed its 2-GW expansion line.

Solar Power World attended the opening of its Greenwood, South Carolina facility last year, reporting on its manufacturing outfit and its unique approach to supply deals that include working with large-scale project developers and utilities to guarantee domestic content supplies.

At RE+, attendees can see its U.S.-manufactured high-efficiency monocrystalline bifacial PERC solar cells and meet with company representatives to discuss domestic sourcing, manufacturing capabilities, and the evolving needs of the U.S. solar market.

“There’s an important difference between talking about building a domestic solar supply chain and actually making it happen,” said ES Foundry CEO Alex Zhu in a statement. “At ES Foundry, we’re manufacturing at commercial scale, shipping cells to customers and navigating the realities of U.S. production every day. That experience gives us a unique perspective on what it will take to build a competitive, sustainable American solar industry. Success requires more than manufacturing capacity. It requires the right economics, technology investments, customer demand and policies to support long-term growth. We’re proud to be delivering on the promise of American manufacturing today while helping shape the industry’s future.”

Visit ES Foundry at RE+ 2026: Booth C7434, November 16–19, Las Vegas, Nevada.

The Wide-Leg Jeans That Every French Girl (And Kate Moss) Owns

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Last month, while taking in the spring/summer 2027 shows at Paris Fashion Week, I was eager to write about the street style trends bubbling up around the showgoers in town. Which proved harder than anticipated. For one thing, the flashiest dressers had been given full looks by the brands whose shows they were being paid to attend, and reporting on those felt like a somewhat dishonest exercise in journalism. For another, the consensus seems to be that we are living in a post-trend world, and fashion people in particular have little interest in dressing like anyone else. And so I turned my attention to real-life Parisians, among whom one item seemed to proliferate: jeans.

No, this isn’t going to be a trend report on something as perennially popular as denim, but on a particular high-waisted, flared style that I spotted so frequently, I was forced to dredge up what little French remains from my GCSEs to ask a woman where hers were from as I caught her climbing onto a Lime bike outside Le Saint-Gervais bar. “Excuse-moi, madame, votre pantalons… le brand?” She paused for a moment, swung her leg over the saddle, and unleashed a torrent of French from which I managed to decipher just two words: “Anna” and “Gérard Darel”, the latter needing no explanation.

Founded in Saint-Germain-des-Prés in the 1970s, the brand has long been a shorthand for what the rest of the world considers the tenets of French-girl style: discreet, easy-to-wear classics with a cultivated air. (So cultivated, in fact, that the brand acquired the original handwritten Proust Questionnaire in 2003, and recently collaborated with the philosopher Alain de Botton on a card-game version.) Which is all very Left Bank, of course, but perhaps the clearest evidence of its credentials is just how commonplace Gérard Darel is in everyday Parisian life. You can scarcely move for its iconic 24H bag—a soft, logo-free shoulder bag that Vogue once named among the bags of the century, alongside the Hermès Birkin – during the school rush on the Métro, where it is generally to be found stuffed with binders and tangled wired headphones.

But back to the jeans–an American invention, admittedly, but then again, this is the land of French fries. What made my startled Parisienne’s look so good? Much of it comes down to the cut–something Kate Moss, a woman who found fame in nothing more than a pair of jeans, understands, having been photographed in the brand’s Anna jeans as early as 2019. Designed by former creative director Valérie Gerbi and named after her daughter Anna – granddaughter of the brand’s founders, Danièle and Gérard Darel–the design braces at just the right point on the hips, fits snugly through the thighs and flares outwards just below the knee, skimming the top of a pair of shoes to create the illusion of longer legs. The double belt loops, meanwhile, criss-cross along the waistband to give the impression of a cinched waist, while the back pockets perform a similarly flattering service for the bum.

And yet the technicalities of a good fit can only explain so much. Perhaps the true appeal was in the way she wore them–with white ballet flats that I’m convinced were from Repetto’s men’s section (the Luciens) and a navy three-quarter-zip jumper with the sleeves pushed up–or the ease with which disappeared into the Marais, hair whipping in the wind.

The Stock Market’s Biggest Risk Isn’t a Crash. It’s What Investors Might Do When One Arrives.

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

A market decline hurts. An improvised response can make it worse.

Imagine seeing your nest egg lose 30% of its value. In this scenario, the market has already delivered a painful setback. Selling may seem like the obvious way to regain control, but selling also creates a new problem: When will you get back into the market?

In reality, panic selling -- and delaying reentering the market -- can lead to enormous opportunity costs for investors. Let's explore why.

Seeing your portfolio lose value is never fun. Indeed, psychological studies have shown that investment losses have a greater psychological impact than equivalent gains, a phenomenon known as loss aversion. Therefore, it's completely understandable that many people seek immediate relief when the market enters a sell-off, correction, or crash.

However, the psychological relief of liquidating investments creates a new problem: determining when "it's safe."

In practice, most investors simply wait too long. Many wait for recessions to end or for bullish headlines to appear. But bullish headlines often lag positive price action. As a result, investors on the sidelines often miss out on massive recovery rallies. As we'll see next, that can have a significant impact on long-term growth.

Consider a $10,000 portfolio. If it drops 30%, its value will fall to $7,000. To get back to $10,000, the portfolio will need to gain $3,000. Since the overall value has fallen, it will need to generate approximately a 43% return on $7,000 to reach $10,000. So to wipe out a 30% drop, it needs to gain 43%. These amounts differ because the starting amounts differ.

Beyond the arithmetic, there are other costs. Transaction costs, lost dividend payments, and taxes have to be considered. In short, selling might ease one type of headache but create several others.

Despite understanding the math and the other implications of selling, many investors can't resist the urge to sell when the stock market is falling. One way to train yourself not to sell is to imagine a similar situation in a different market.

For example, let's say you own a house. Now imagine that another house in your neighborhood sells for half of what might normally be expected. That sale will decrease the value of all homes in the neighborhood, perhaps significantly. But even if the price of your own home falls 10%, 15%, or even 25%, that doesn't mean one of the bedrooms in your home disappears.

Stock portfolios work the same way. Prices can fall, but that doesn't necessarily mean that the businesses behind the stocks have deteriorated to the same degree.

For a buy-and-hold strategy to work, investors must build a portfolio that can withstand periods of volatility. Leveraged positions and meme stocks can wreck a portfolio during a stock market correction or crash. Therefore, savvy investors know to limit their exposure, or, better yet, avoid such investments altogether. By building a diversified portfolio based around well-run businesses, an investor can avoid much of the worst effects of a market downturn.

Market crashes are terrible, but thankfully, they're also rare. However, to prepare for them, it's best to have a plan. Writing it down can help you manage your emotions, think carefully, and avoid panic. At the end of the day, the math and the studies are clear: It's best to ride out market downturns rather than sell and try to time reentry perfectly.

If you can keep a cool head and remember this in the moment, you'll likely come through the next financial downturn in better shape than many other investors.

8 Soulslikes With Insane Difficulty Spikes

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

Christian Bognar is a Contributor at DualShockers with over three years of experience covering the gaming industry. Since 2022, he has written guides and reviews for publications including DualShockers, CBR, Attack of the Fanboy, PC Invasion, and The Game Slayer. He holds a Bachelor’s Degree in Business Administration

The Soulslikes genre offers some of the greatest challenges in gaming. With difficult bosses, enemies that demand patience, and many obstacles throughout a level, meeting success in any Soulslike can be incredibly rewarding.

However, there are times when a game takes it too far and includes a harsh difficulty spike that seemingly comes out of nowhere. This could sometimes lead to areas of the game feeling a bit unfair.

It's not the genre's area of expertise, but some of its representatives are more daring, offering fascinating movement mechanics.

While the games listed in this article are some of the best entries in the genre, they do have pacing issues when it comes to difficulty. Here are eight Soulslikes with brutal difficulty spikes.

Sekiro is already a demanding game from the get-go, but its difficulty reaches a whole other level in the mid to late game. Enemies become more aggressive, minibosses hit harder, and all encounters will constantly remind players that they need to master the parry system to stand a chance.

Boss fights also become considerably more demanding, requiring players to learn lengthy attack patterns and maintain constant pressure rather than relying on dodging. Just when you thought you were getting good at the game, Sekiro: Shadows Die Twice raises the bar.

The expansion of Elden Ring, Shadow of the Erdtree, is known for its difficulty spike right from the start. As soon as you enter the Land of Shadow, you’ll realize enemies deal way more damage, and your character is much weaker overall.

With the introduction of Scadutree Fragments, items that raise attack power and defense, players essentially have to start from scratch and engage with a new progression system. In other words, even OP base-game builds get a wake-up call when starting the DLC.

Overall, Lies of P is generally on the easier side for a Soulslike. However, the game has brutal difficulty spikes in a handful of its bosses. For example, Laxasia the Complete, Simon Manus, and King of Puppets are ten times more challenging than anything else in the game.

Each of these bosses is a true test of reflexes and endurance, where even the smallest mistake can prove costly. What makes these encounters particularly jarring is the stark contrast in difficulty, as the areas leading up to them are relatively easy to breeze through.

There’s no denying that The First Berserker: Khazan is a very challenging game right from its first level. Still, despite it being a very tough experience through and through, it still features an insane difficulty spike in its second half that can sometimes feel pretty unfair.

Late-game bosses are some of the most demanding encounters the genre has to offer, featuring relentless combos that leave very little room for mistakes. Khazan’s combat is super satisfying, but players who haven’t mastered its mechanics will quickly discover just how unforgiving it can be.

If you're looking for the heir to Soulsborne games, look no further than this list of ten Soulslikes that are unexpected masterpieces.

While the 2023 version of Lords of the Fallen never reaches the difficulty heights of 2014’s, it’s definitely not an easy experience. The game reaches its peak in difficulty during Bramis Castle, an area filled with the hardest enemies in the entire game.

Bramis Castle has been heavily regarded as unfair by many players due to the absurd amount of elite enemies. It was so hard that there were multiple post-launch patches to make it feel more reasonable. Still, despite the patches to make it easier, Bramis Castle remains an insane difficulty spike.

The original Hollow Knight was a fairly well-balanced experience from beginning to end, and while Silksong is a phenomenal game, it’s not as consistent as the original. For example, Bilewater increases the difficulty tenfold due to poisonous waters and brutal platforming.

Additionally, Act 3 of Silksong introduces an entirely new set of challenges as the Void corrupts Pharloom, making enemies considerably more dangerous while changing areas and levels. Fortunately, Act 3 is completely optional, meaning players who aren’t interested in tackling this difficulty spike can simply ignore it.

Wuchang: Fallen Feathers features several notoriously sharp difficulty spikes, mainly in its boss fights. Wuchang has the tendency to make the player feel incredibly powerful while exploring a level and then completely ripping that feeling away during some of the main bosses.

One of the earliest examples of this is the Commander Honglan boss fight, the first brick wall players will hit in a playthrough. All earlier bosses are a walk in the park compared to Commander Honglan, and everything that follows involves bosses that demand fast reflexes and perfect performance.

Nioh 3's difficulty spikes can be particularly unforgiving, especially when players enter the Heian Period. This period involves some of the most challenging locations and bosses in the game, such as Ibaraki Doji, Kajiwara Kagetoki, and many more.

Pretty much, once the player enters the Heian Period, the real Nioh 3 experience begins, and the rest of the adventure is a true test of skill. Bosses hit hard, are faster, and the importance of leveling equipment increases dramatically.

There's nothing more fitting for this season than being destroyed by the same boss fifty-seven times in a row.

David Duchovny on Books, Band and ‘The X-Files’

📅 Nov 16, 2026  |  Category: Business  |  Read Source →

David Duchovny has been famous for as long as I have been alive. From 1993 to 2002, The X-Files made government conspiracies a staple of primetime television. Duchovny’s FBI special agent Fox Mulder, with his skeptical counterpart, Gillian Anderson’s agent Dana Scully, personified the imminent breakdown of consensus reality in the coming century, delivering the paranoid fringe into 20 million American living rooms. The show belongs to a world of monoculture that no longer exists, and it launched Duchovny into a realm of visibility that’s hard to imagine now, when famous people are produced and consumed at an industrial rate. A few weeks ago, as my boyfriend shopped for a suit jacket at Crowley Vintage in Brooklyn and I edited this story on my phone, I watched a college-age boy put on a long, gray trench coat and announce to his girlfriend: “Mulder mode.”

Duchovny went on to star in Californication, a raunchy dramedy about a divorced novelist navigating the sexually and professionally parasitic Hollywood studio system of the 2000s. Many viewers interpreted Californication as a kind of autofiction, although Duchovny did not write for the show. The actor has written a number of novels, but it’s apparent to anyone who has spent 30 seconds with him that this soft-spoken, reluctant celebrity has nothing to do with the character Hank Moody. Still, Californication did anticipate something about art, celebrity, and self-commodification: Anybody who writes today must contend with marketing an artistic persona for a public that does not, as a rule, read.

In addition to his six books, Duchovny has released four records, and written and directed a number of feature films. He greets the degraded state of American arts with a charming blend of rigor and self-deprecation. “I’m not Jonathan Franzen,” he says of his fiction-writing process. “But if I work on something every day, it deepens. And eventually, hopefully, I can achieve one tenth of what Jonathan Franzen achieves.” But he has an aversion to the sort of publicity that might make his various artistic ventures more lucrative. He tells me he finds it “spiritually impoverishing.”

I meet Duchovny with his band—millennial slackers half his age—at their practice space in Carroll Gardens, where they’re preparing to tour their fourth LP, Prince of Pieces. He wears a white Hanes T-shirt, tight underneath his athletic arms, and he has a tattoo on his ankle—a compass pointing west, something he got when his daughter, West, was hospitalized as an infant. (She’s 27 now, an accomplished actor. “She wanted to be a doctor,” he says. “I was so happy when she told me she was going to pursue acting. Thank God.”)

The albums are composed of subdued, easy-to-listen-to folk-rock tracks, not dissimilar from early Kurt Vile. It’s the band’s first rehearsal in ages, and one of their only chances to prepare in advance of a European tour. In person, they play the songs harder and heavier, sprinkling in crowd-pleasing covers: “Godzilla” by Blue Öyster Cult, “The One I Love” by REM, and—“sadly only one time,” Duchovny says—“Hotline Bling.” The energy in the room is high. These men like one another. Duchovny’s manager offers me a beer from the cooler I’m perched on. I gesture to the tallboy in my purse. He looks disappointed. “You didn’t trust us,” he says. Duchovny steps aside to take a sip of water and picks up the microphone.

“You can sing along,” his keyboardist, Colin Lee, says.

At 66, David Duchovny remains a sex symbol. He’s in better shape than he was on The X-Files. He’s in better shape than the boys in the band. The night after practice, during a Q&A for a screening of his 2023 film Reverse the Curse, he mentions that the wardrobe department had to fashion a “BDSM harness” for him to wear under his clothes to remind him to hunch like an old man. He plays a Red Sox fan who bonds with his estranged son over baseball, marijuana, and their shared lust for a beautiful, young death doula. Duchovny’s character has terminal cancer. “I’m in my dying era,” he tells the crowd. “I die in every film. ”

For the most part, the audience at Westchester County’s Irvington Theater is old enough to have watched The X-Files as it aired. At least two esoteric-looking old men carry David’s poetry collection like a prayer book. There are a handful of young people. A college-aged girl in a KN95 mask raises her hand to ask about Bucking Fucking Dent, the novel adaptation of the Reverse the Curse screenplay, which Duchovny wrote while the movie was in production purgatory. “In the book, Ted and Marty’s last name is Fullilove. In the movie you change it to Fullaker. Why did you do that?”

“Good question,” Duchovny says, stalling. “Full of love. Full of shit. It’s all the same.”

Inevitably, someone asks about the new X-Files. Ryan Coogler is rebooting the show for Hulu. Duchovny will later suggest to me that Chris Carter, the show’s original writer, has designs on a reboot of his own—“He has plans, and wants to end with the original show.” As for Coogler’s version, he says, “I know he said he wants me to come do something, but we haven’t figured out what yet.” During the audience Q&A, Duchovny seems to have misheard the question, and spoils the ending for The Exiles, his forthcoming Taiwanese crime film: “I die in that one too.”

Duchovny’s latest book, About Time, a collection of poems published last year by Akashic, is also largely about dying: the death of his father, the death of his first marriage, the ambient death that hung in the air at the beginning of the decade. He started writing the poems when his therapist encouraged him to talk to dead people. “He was talking about Rudolph Steiner’s belief that you can talk to the dead. And he said: You can talk to your father and you should. My dad and I didn’t connect like we might have,” Duchovny says. “When you’re younger, you think you have all the time in the world to get the relationship you want. But the relationship doesn’t end.” In conversation, he is cerebral without being annoying. “There’s a power struggle when you read a really good novel,” he says. “Somebody’s mind is taking over your mind. I think about language a lot and how we take it for granted as being an accurate signifier. When I was younger I thought communication was possible. I’m not so sure now. But it’s fun to try.”

Duchovny’s humility complicates the sexual charisma that still lives at the center of his public persona. His introduction to About Time begins, “I know what you’re thinking: Just what the world needs now—a bunch of poems from an actor.” There’s a sense that he wants to get ahead of the reader’s skepticism. But there’s also plenty of perceptive lines in the poems themselves: “You dreamt you wrote something so good, / so ambivalent and destructive, so creative, / so lacking in morals, decency, or instruction, / so sad and so funny, / so useless and old, / so familiar and new, / so full of theft and power / that they had to name a god after it.” I ask him if good writing is necessarily amoral. He says, “Probably. Which is why any politically correct work of art is usually super successful and really bad. Usually artists are difficult people, and I have no problem separating the art from the artist. But people do now.”

In college, Duchovny wanted to be a poet. “I thought it was an actual occupation you could have,” he says. When an underclassman turned up in his workshop who was better than him, he quit writing poems on the spot. “It was actually Walter Kirn,” he says. “He showed up at 16-years-old. He was a genius. I was like okay—I’m not a poet.”

The two men have become close in the last decade. Kirn, who wrote Up in the Air and founded the newspaper County Highway, tells me what Duchovny was like at Princeton: “He was golden. He had an aura. I was a little starstruck by him.” Duchovny has agreed to play paranormal radio host Art Bell in American Nights, a biopic that Kirn is developing alongside director Alex Lee Moyer. Bell’s Coast to Coast, a cult AM show during the ’80s and ’90s, was beloved by long-haul truckers and compound preppers alike, who called in to report UFO sightings, cattle mutilations, and crop circles. “I, like the rest of my generation, grew up on The X-Files,” Moyer tells me. “It made a massive impression on me, and I’ve been a fan of David ever since. The synchronicity of him being a part of this is not lost on me.”

I ask Duchovny if he always needed to be the best. “I at least needed to be the best in my class,” he says.

In LA, I meet Duchovny in a quiet restaurant to talk about his books. He describes his first novel as “an act of cowardice.” Holy Cow is narrated by an anthropomorphized barnyard animal who uncovers the conspiracy theory that is factory farming. “I thought: Well, they’re not gonna cut me off at the knees on this. Because I’m clearly not that serious. It was calculated. I didn’t want to try for the great American novel the first time as an actor.” The books that followed are more ambitious, each one better than the last. By the time he was promoting his fourth book, 2021’s Truly Like Lightning, he made his ambition plain. “I want to be taken seriously,” he said on Marc Maron’s podcast. “That’s my weakness. I want that book to be taken seriously.”

“You don’t want people to be like the guy from The X-Files wrote a book,” Maron said.

“That’s what they’re gonna say,” Duchovny said, “but I hope and I believe that eventually people will see that I mean business.”

Truly Like Lightning centers on a Mormon polygamist family forced to enter the godless world of Los Angeles when their homestead is upended. “When I started to read about Mormonism, it struck me as super optimistic. Everyone’s gonna get their own planet and everyone’s gonna be able to propagate souls. As opposed to a European perspective, which was always about the end of something, being 2,000 years removed from this thing. The Mormons said no, this is still happening.” The novel takes cues from the literary critic Harold Bloom, who wrote that Mormonism was an “American original” and called its founder a “religious genius.” Duchovny studied under Bloom at Yale, where he earned some of an English PhD. “Everything goes back to Bloom,” he says. “And you never really leave grad school. I’m not sure I’ve left grad school. They might still be waiting for my dissertation.”

But Duchovny did leave grad school. He signed with a talent agent one summer to try to earn some money acting in commercials. His agent told him that if he wanted to go out for theater or film roles, he’d have to get into an acting class. “I said: I’m not doing anything. When I stepped into acting class, something happened. I wasn’t a demonstrative person—I was an intellectual person. So it was like: Yeah, be unreasonable. Be irrational. It opened up a different world, a world of fiction, a world without consequences. I’m doing a scene, and I’m yelling at this person and I’m going to murder them, but I don’t have to go to jail.”

For someone with half a dozen good books and an enormous public profile, it’s surprising there haven’t been more reviews. Johnny Temple, who published Duchovny’s two most recent books on Akashic, tells me, “It’s certainly frustrating that there hasn't been more review coverage of David’s books. For any well-known actor, musician, or other type of public figure, there’s always going to be obstacles to being taken seriously in a different medium.” Kirn tells me, “That makes writing fiction a much purer achievement in my mind. You’re doing this because you love it, and you know that two thirds of your fans will only remember you as a TV character. It’s a brilliant, Sisyphean task. Also, he’s a superb writer.”

I ask Duchovny whether he still thinks about being the best. “I think now I can acknowledge that it’s a hollow designation,” he says. “Especially in the arts. Even though we give awards, which is silly. Holy Cow made the Times bestseller list. Ever since, everything I write sells less and less.”

“It’s hard for someone to champion it,” he adds. “If I wrote a memoir, I’m sure it would get reviewed in the Times.”

Talking to Duchovny, one is reminded that wanting to be taken seriously and being in on the joke aren’t mutually exclusive. As band practice winds down in Brooklyn, the guys are excited to cover Lou Reed’s “Perfect Day.” Duchovny crosses his arms in front of his lap like a schoolboy as he sings. To my ear it sounds just fine, but Lee, the keyboardist, asks the mixer to lower the reverb on David’s voice.

“Maybe lower David’s mic in general?” the bassist, Mitchell Stewart asks.

“Lower than zero,” Duchovny says. “Less than zero.”

A few days later, he texts me. He just screened an early cut of Soapbox, a Hollywood satire he wrote and stars in opposite Laverne Cox. At first, he’s bummed that the test audience has decided that his character shouldn’t get killed in the final moments of the movie. But a couple hours later, he writes me again: “People don’t want me to die. I guess I should be flattered.”
